# PART 1 — INSTAGRAM (112 NEW)

# Instagram DEEP-MATH pull — 2026-10-30 (slot DEEPMATH-IG200)

**Running count — dissected this pull: 112 NEW posts. Target was 100 (revised from 200 by Garrett's spec change, 17:25 CT); passed by 12 because every selected candidate cleared the quality gate — the standing sweep rule values completeness over stopping at the number.** Cumulative IG dissected total: 383 + 112 = **495**.

**The bar (Garrett):** the 350-page-NumPy-PDF class of find — deep mathematics, deep learning, statistical analysis, university/lab research, lecture notes, textbooks, long-form technical PDFs, datasets, one-of-a-kind systems. NFL analytics is home territory; anything deep that can transform GSE, the engine, the fleet, or the autonomous workflow counts. **Mission lens (17:29 CT):** GSE is a research and data company proving its mathematics on sports — every dossier carries a **Transfer beyond sports** field, and physics-of-sport finds (drag/Magnus/trajectory/fluid dynamics/physics-informed ML) carry a ⚛ PHYSICS PRIORITY flag and jumped the queue.

**Route disclosure (honest):** this run had no live browser, so the ranked For You scroll was replaced by (a) `instagram-cli posts` grid harvests of 38 curated deep-math/ML/statistics/sports-analytics accounts, (b) `social.search` topic rounds (arXiv/papers, lecture-notes/PDFs, NFL models, Bayesian/statistics, sports physics), and (c) the academic aperture Garrett opened — arXiv PDFs resolved directly. **1,197 unique NEW candidate posts surfaced; 112 dissected.** Dedupe: every candidate's shortcode checked against the seen index + all prior reports + the consolidated master index + the shared-items register (384 known shortcodes) before selection. **Zero real 429s** — one stop-check trip was the known false-positive class (bare "429" digits inside an FBID in an otherwise valid 58-post response; the file parsed clean, no provider error). Comments were not analyzed (standing rule). No likes, saves, follows, DMs, sign-ups, or contact with any creator — read-only throughout.

## Top finds first

1. **The 698-page CMU mathematics book (DM via @quantscience_ DfH-MZ9G8oS) — ADOPT-CANDIDATE.** A complete doctoral-level text on mathematical thinking and proof, free from CMU, downloaded and page-verified. This is the NumPy-PDF class exactly: the kind of foundational text the fleet's reasoning layer should be trained against. Transfer: proof discipline is domain-independent — it is the difference between an engine that asserts and an engine that derives.
2. **Petersen & Zech, "Mathematical theory of deep learning," 333 pp (DcK_pItlmol) — ADOPT-CANDIDATE.** The carousel literally posts pages of the arXiv monograph. Approximation theory for neural networks, treated at research level. Transfer: the same approximation/error-bound machinery governs any function-fitting the engine does, sports or not.
3. **autoarxiv — paper → running code (DeUTUj7jd5M) — TEST.** Swap arxiv → autoarxiv in a paper URL and an agent implements it. That is the ingest→implement step of Garrett's autonomous workflow as a product. First test: point it at one engine-relevant paper already in the corpus and grade the output against the paper's own tables.
4. **Jev-style scoring vs LLM decoding, explained (De67QXDAC3R) — TEST.** A mainstream DS account now teaches the exact logit-scoring pattern of the Jev family — the technology just auditioned (Laya) and queued (Simple Jev, S110) for the engine's judge seat. The explainer's taxonomy (when typed logit scoring beats free generation) is directly citable in the judge-seat design file.
5. **Xiaomi's 7,000+ open RL training environments (DfBys9PjWsG) — TEST.** Open weights + a large environment suite matters more than another model drop: environments are what agents learn *in*. For GSE: simulation environments are how the engine's agents could train against synthetic game states. Transfer: RL environment design is domain-general (robotics, operations, games).
6. **Cushing & Stewart lottery designs (CvSlMePAUsa) — LEARN-ONLY, method transfers.** Constraint programming over hypergraph covering designs, from a Matt Parker video to a Manchester paper. The GSE transfer is concrete: covering-design math is the math of guaranteeing coverage with minimum tickets — the same structure as coverage-optimal bet cards and lineup portfolios.
7. **@eulerism_ stochastic-calculus textbook carousels (DbDDOiFCGiW) — LEARN-ONLY.** A serialized textbook (SDEs, measure theory) taught through carousels with MIT/Stanford/Cambridge framing. The account is a standing source for the engine's simulation-layer foundations.
8. **The Bayesian cluster (DembKCFOKLc hierarchical models · DeC3r4UJ-ZC Think Bayesian · DcFwfTvEXX7 MCMC sampling · DdClIrnoxxc "How does NUTS actually sample" · DekQevgJki4 confidence vs credible intervals) — LEARN-ONLY, one shelf.** Five independent posts converging on the engine's inference layer: partial pooling for player/team strength, NUTS as the sampler inside Stan/PyMC, and the credible-interval language the reasoning trail should use when it states uncertainty.
9. **⚛ BEYOND MAGNUS — "The Other Physics of Pitch Movement" (DSTNh6ygZ2W) — LEARN-ONLY, physics priority.** Seam-shifted-wake territory: the pitch-movement physics beyond the textbook Magnus story. Baseball is not the engine's sport, but this is the proof pattern for physics-of-sport modeling — and the same wake physics governs a spiraling football's flight and a punt's carry.
10. **17 Python libraries that "open the hedge fund black box" (DfFZLdgj_RK) — LEARN-ONLY.** Named with links: OpenBB, vollib, pysabr (SABR stochastic-volatility), FinancePy, gs-quant, tf-quant-finance, optlib, and ten more. The quant shelf is the sports market's closest cousin; SABR/volatility machinery is directly relevant to modeling line movement as a stochastic process.
11. **PaperScorer (De89y-wJkQk) — WATCH.** A model that predicts paper acceptance, 50 pp, downloaded. For a fleet that ingests hundreds of papers, a calibrated accept/quality signal is a triage layer — watched, not trusted, because a predictability model can entrench exactly the biases the corpus hunt tries to escape.
12. **The NGS 2026 metric-expansion cluster (@nextgenstats, 3 dossiers) — LEARN-ONLY.** Run Scheme Classification and the NGS Draft Model posts document the league's own tracking-data feature factory expanding. These are the feature definitions the engine's tracking layer should mirror, with provenance.

The infographic visualizes Bayes’ theorem as a step‑by‑step “before‑and‑after” of a medical test. On the left, a sea of grey human icons (the *prior* population) includes a few yellow figures that represent individuals who might be infected. A central clipboard declares “Virus Detected,” symbolizing a positive test result. An arrow points rightward, indicating the logical transition from prior belief to updated belief. On the right, the same population is reduced, and the previously yellow figures are now boxed in green, signifying the *posterior* – the subset identified after applying the test evidence. The bottom caption, “Post = (Prior)(Bayes factor)” and “Bayes rule redesigned,” distills the mathematical core: posterior probability equals prior probability multiplied by the Bayes factor (likelihood ratio).

He answers the question 'What is the benefit of the uncertainty in just the Bayesian model?' explaining that under the Bayesian framework you can apply intervals to parameters and state with a certain level of probability (e.g., P(θ ∈ [L,U]) = 0.95) that the parameter lies within the interval. In contrast, under the frequentist framework the parameter is fixed and unknown, so the probability that it falls within any given confidence interval is either zero or one — the interval either has the parameter or it doesn't. Summary: This is Part 2 of 7 from 'Pitch AI - Field Notes' on pitch design and ball flight, titled 'Why Do Pitches Move?' The video explains that pitch movement results from three forces acting on the ball toward home plate: drag (slows it), gravity (pulls down), and spin/Magnus force (pushes depending on spin direction). It details how spin direction sets the break — backspin creates ride (4-seam), topspin creates drop (curve), and tilted spin creates run (sinker) — from a right-hander's view. It distinguishes 'useful spin' (axis across flight) from 'gyro spin' (spins like a bullet), noting MLB four-seams average about 90% spin efficiency. Hand position affects this: supinators achieve efficient backspin, pronators get more gyro. The video then explains that backspin creates Magnus lift, pushing air down behind the ball and reducing its drop, though it still falls. This reduction is measured as 'induced vertical break' (IVB), with the MLB four-seam average around 16 inches, above average at 18 inches, and elite ride at 20+ inches. Summary: A presenter in a red shirt narrates the scientific history of the baseball curveball. He explains that the pitch was once considered controversial and 'shameful' in youth baseball. In 1941, Life Magazine investigated with high-speed photography and concluded the ball does not actually curve sideways but follows a straight path and drops due to gravity, appearing to curve because slower pitches drop more than fastballs. Decades later, Life revisited the topic with improved technology, hiring photographer Jean Millie and pitcher Ken Raffensberger, who painted a ball half black to track spin. They found the ball spun at about 1,400 RPM. MIT aeronautics professor Joseph Bicknell then used a wind tunnel to spin a baseball at 1,400 RPM in a 43-MPH wind, confirming the ball travels in a gentle arc. The presenter notes this is the Magnus Effect — the force causing curveballs — first demonstrated by physicist Heinrich Gustav Magnus in 1852. It opens by stating every ball you throw falls due to gravity, yet batters swear a fastball can rise over their bat. The video answers 'No' — it doesn't rise, it just falls less than the batter's brain expects. Using an example of a batter named Leo in a big game, it shows he sees the ball at belt height and swings. The explanation reveals the secret is backspin. The spinning ball drags air downward, and the air pushes the ball up — the Magnus effect. This upward lift is weaker than gravity, so the ball still falls, but a typical pro fastball drops about 40 cm (16 inches) less than a pitch with no spin. Because the pitch arrives in about 0.40 seconds — too fast to track visually — the batter's brain predicts its path based on past pitches. - **WHAT (from the post's own text):** Turn any paper into running code 🚀

Just swap arxiv → autoarxiv in the paper URL.

That hands the paper to an AI agent from alphaXiv. It reads the abstract, the claims, and the linked GitHub repo, then clones the codebase and works through the usual setup pain: dependencies, broken paths, environment config, and hardware assumptions.

From there it designs a minimal reproduction. A smaller model, fewer steps, and a single GPU instead of a cluster, scaled down just enough to test whether the headline claim holds.

The whole run is live and fully logged. Loss curves, metrics, and training progress are all observable as it happens.

What comes back is a clean signal on whether the minimal run matches the paper's reported result, plus an estimate of what a full replication would cost in compute and time.

A lot of research code dies in setup before anyone verifies a single number. This moves reproduction from a weekend of debugging to a URL change.

Pick a paper and try it now.

👉 Over to you: Which paper would you replicate first?

### DM-059 — @dailydoseofds_ — De_N5k3iel7

- **URL:** https://www.instagram.com/p/De_N5k3iel7/ · likes 199 · source: acct_dailydoseofds_.json
- **WHAT (from the post's own text):** Vector search is not always the answer!

A 30-year-old algorithm with zero training, zero embeddings, and zero fine-tuning still powers Elasticsearch, OpenSearch, and most production search systems today.

It's called BM25, and it's worth understanding why it refuses to die.

Say you're searching for "transformer attention mechanism" in a library of ML papers. BM25 scores documents using three core ideas:

1️⃣ Word rarity matters more than word frequency
Every paper contains "the" and "is" so those words carry no signal. But "transformer" is specific and informative, so BM25 gives it a much higher weight.

2️⃣ Repetition helps, but with diminishing returns
If "attention" appears 10 times in a paper, that's a strong relevance signal. But the jump from 10 to 100 occurrences barely moves the score. BM25 applies a saturation curve to prevent keyword stuffing from gaming the results.

3️⃣ Document length gets normalized
A 50-page paper will naturally contain more keyword hits than a 5-page paper. BM25 adjusts for this so longer documents don't dominate rankings just because they have more

It runs a multi-agent pipeline that does the thinking for you:

➡️ Retriever pulls relevant reference diagrams
➡️ Planner drafts the figure spec

➡️ Critic loops on the rendered image until it matches your source

➡️ Generate one diagram from a .txt or PDF

➡️ Build a full figure package from one paper, with figures.tex and captions.md included
➡️ Score diagrams against a human reference on faithfulness, readability, and aesthetics

### DM-062 — @dailydoseofds_ — De1xqWHAR4e

- **URL:** https://www.instagram.com/reel/De1xqWHAR4e/ · likes 204 · source: acct_dailydoseofds_.json
- **WHAT (from the post's own text):** NVIDIA found a way to make KV cache transferable between models ⚡

The target model skips prefill entirely, and conversion runs 2.7 to 25x faster than reprocessing the context.

Here's why this matters.

LLM APIs are stateless, so every turn sends the entire conversation back to the model. Prompt caching lets providers hold the KV cache and bill hits at 10% of the base input rate. That 90% reduction is one of the biggest levers in LLM serving.

But the cache only works on the model that produced it. If traffic moves to another model for cost or capability reasons, the accumulated KV cache becomes invalid and the entire context is billed at full rate again.

NVIDIA's paper treats this as a representation problem. Transferring the cache means converting one model's representation into what another model expects.

For Qwen3 14B → 32B, a single source layer reconstructed 56% of the variance in the target model's keys. Using the top eight source layers per target layer pushes that to 79%.

The mapper has three parts:

➡️ Each target layer and head gets its own linear map, solved in closed

### DM-063 — @dailydoseofds_ — DeRujxsDIGH

- **URL:** https://www.instagram.com/reel/DeRujxsDIGH/ · likes 72 · source: acct_dailydoseofds_.json
- **WHAT (from the post's own text):** Why does KV cache store K/V vectors but never Q? 🧠

A popular technical LLM interview question.

LLMs are autoregressive, so each token is predicted from tokens before it. To understand why KV cache only stores K and V, we need to trace exactly how the last hidden state is produced.

Let's walk through this with a 10-token prompt.

1️⃣ Prefill
All 10 tokens go through the model in one parallel forward pass with causal masking. At every layer, each position produces a query, key, and value vector. This pass is compute-heavy and is why the first token takes noticeably longer. TTFT is mostly prefill.

2️⃣ The first output token
To generate the 11th token, only the 10th token's hidden state is needed. It's projected to vocab-dim, goes through softmax and sampling, and produces token 11.

3️⃣ The attention matrix
QKᵀ for a 10-token prompt gives a 10×10 matrix. Row 10 is Q₁₀·K₁, Q₁₀·K₂, all the way to Q₁₀·K₁₀.

Notice that only Q₁₀ appears in it. Q₁ through Q₉ only belong to their corresponding rows, and those hidden states were already discarded because they were never needed again.

### DM-066 — @dailydoseofds_ — De06uHEiJv4

- **URL:** https://www.instagram.com/p/De06uHEiJv4/ · likes 174 · source: acct_dailydoseofds_.json
- **WHAT (from the post's own text):** The next step after Karpathy's autoresearch idea 🧠

Tuning an agent is mostly manual work: editing prompts, tools, and control flow by hand, then rerunning evals to see what moved.

Researchers have been building automated optimizers to do that outer loop instead. The underlying process is the same: an LLM proposes a change, an evaluator scores it, and the proposer reads that result before proposing the next one.

They differ in what they edit and what feedback they get to read.

1️⃣ GEPA (Berkeley) - Optimizes the text of a system like prompts, tool descriptions, or the agent's own code. Instead of collapsing a run into one scalar reward the way RL does, it reads the full execution trace, diagnoses why the run failed, and proposes a targeted fix. Converges in hundreds of rollouts instead of the thousands GRPO needs.

2️⃣ AutoResearch (inspired by Karpathy) - Runs a narrower version of the loop where a coding agent iterates on a program.md file, scores outputs against fixed evals, and keeps whatever improves.

3️⃣ Meta-Harness - Points the loop at the harness itself, the scaffolding

- **WHAT (from the post's own text):** 5 embedding compression techniques, clearly explained 🧠

Must-know for AI engineers.

Ten million 1,536-dimensional embeddings will occupy:

➡️ 62 GB in float32
➡️ 15 GB in int8
➡️ 2 GB as packed bits

That only covers the raw vector payload. An in-memory system also needs space for the ANN index, metadata, and allocator overhead.

Embedding compression works along two axes: the number of dimensions stored, and the number of bits used for each dimension.

1️⃣ PCA
A post-training transform. It learns the directions with the most variance from a representative sample, then projects existing embeddings into a smaller space. Works with any embedding model, but the projection must be applied consistently to indexed vectors and queries.

2️⃣ MRL
Alters the training objective so that selected prefixes of the embedding remain useful independently. You can truncate at inference without training a separate model per target dimension. OpenAI reports text-embedding-3-large at 256 dimensions still outperforms the 1,536-dimensional ada-002 on MTEB.

3️⃣ Scalar quantization
Keeps dimensions fixed a

- **WHAT (from the post's own text):** Train your own LLM from scratch 🧠

On a free Google Colab in 1 day.

This repo builds a GPT-style transformer from the ground up, without using any high-level libraries.

You see exactly how attention, multi-head attention, the feed-forward block, embeddings, residuals, and layer norm fit together.

And it doesn't stop at the model. It walks the whole path from raw data to generated text:

➡️ Data download, preprocessing, training, and generation
➡️ Training data from The Pile (825GB across 22 sources)
➡️ Tokenized with tiktoken (r50k_base) and stored in HDF5
➡️ Training loop with eval, LR decay, and crash-safe checkpoints
➡️ An SFT and RLHF guide for what comes after pretraining

The same code scales by changing a few config values. Around 13M parameters is where the output starts producing correct grammar and spelling, and you can train that in about a day on a free Colab or Kaggle T4.

If you've ever wanted to actually see how a transformer works instead of importing one, this is a clean place to start.

### DM-072 — @dailydoseofds_ — DfEXf4ZgbHM

- **URL:** https://www.instagram.com/p/DfEXf4ZgbHM/ · likes 101 · source: acct_dailydoseofds_.json
- **WHAT (from the post's own text):** 5 vector DB indexing methods, clearly explained (with visuals) 🔍

A basic nearest-neighbor search compares the query with every stored vector.

That cost grows linearly with the size of your database, and it becomes impractical across millions of candidates.

Every vector index cuts this work differently: clustering, graph traversal, compression, or a mix of these.

Here are five common approaches:

1️⃣ Flat: exact search that scans every vector, great when the dataset is small enough to scan
2️⃣ IVF: clusters the space and searches only the nearest clusters; more probes = better recall, more latency
3️⃣ HNSW: a multilayer graph that jumps far on top and refines below; low latency, high recall, extra memory
4️⃣ IVF-PQ: IVF + product quantization; compact codes cut memory and compute, with some recall loss
5️⃣ ScaNN: partitions, scores quantized vectors, then rescores the top candidates exactly; built for throughput

The choice depends on what your system can afford to trade: recall, latency, or memory.

We've broken each one down with diagrams in the carousel. Save it for your next R

### DM-073 — @dailydoseofds_ — DetMVtSinZx

- **URL:** https://www.instagram.com/p/DetMVtSinZx/ · likes 115 · source: acct_dailydoseofds_.json
- **WHAT (from the post's own text):** A popular ML interview question 🧠

Which model do you prefer?

Modern neural networks can be misleading. They are overconfident in their predictions.

Here's an experiment using the CIFAR-100 dataset to compare LeNet (1998) with ResNet (2016):

LeNet:
➡️ Accuracy = ~0.55
➡️ Average confidence = ~0.54

ResNet:
➡️ Accuracy = ~0.7
➡️ Average confidence = ~0.9

Despite being more accurate, ResNet is overconfident. While the model thinks it's 90% confident, in reality it only turns out to be 70% accurate.

Calibration solves this.

A model is calibrated if the predicted probabilities align with the actual outcomes. If a model predicts an event with 70% probability, then out of 100 such predictions, ~70 should result in the event.

If you don't care about probabilities, this shouldn't bother you. But if you do, handling this is important because the model will be used in decision-making.

An overly confident model that isn't equally accurate can be highly misleading.

For example: a government hospital wants to conduct an expensive medical test on patients. A reliable probability estimate

- **WHAT (from the post's own text):** An LLM generates text one token at a time, and each token depends on the keys and values of every previous one, stored in the KV cache. Older systems reserved one contiguous slab per request sized for max context length, and most of it sat empty.

Paged attention splits the cache into small fixed-size blocks scattered anywhere in GPU memory, with a lookup table tracking where they live. Watch the clip for the full breakdown.

Link in bio: Fast & Efficient LLM Inference with vLLM

- **WHAT (from the post's own text):** Featured in The Batch this week: what if an agent treated a wrong answer as a to-do list instead of a dead end? 📰📝

That's the idea behind AREX, a research agent that checks its own work one requirement at a time.

✅ Keeps the parts it has already confirmed
🔍 Turns the missing pieces into its next search
🎯 Rates its own confidence before deciding to stop, refine, or start over

One surprise: A small 4B-parameter model trained this way beat a much larger untrained model on 5 of 6 benchmarks. 💡

Full story at the link in bio. 🔗

Every dossier therefore carries a **Transfer beyond sports** field, and physics-of-sport finds were prioritized to the front of the queue.

## A. Physics of sport — the priority tier (15)

### A1. The Seam-Shifted Wake — @PitchingNinja (1181590629698199552) — LEARN-ONLY ★

- **What it is:** The canonical public explanation of the seam-shifted wake (SSW): a pitch's seams, held in a fixed orientation, trip the boundary layer asymmetrically and produce a wake that pushes the ball sideways — movement **not** explained by the Magnus effect, which is why some changeups and sinkers break "the wrong way" relative to their spin.
- **Citation/links:** baseballaero.com baseball aerodynamics main page (the Barton Smith / @NotRealCertain team's research hub), linked in-post.
- **How to use it / system mapping:** This is the physics layer underneath all ball-flight work: any trajectory model that only integrates gravity + drag + Magnus is misspecified for seam-active pitches. Maps to the fleet's physics lane (trajectory simulation) as the correction term to test first.
- **Transfer beyond sports:** Boundary-layer tripping by surface texture is general aerodynamics — the same physics governs any spinning or non-spinning projectile with surface features (industrial sprays, sports projectiles, drone rotors at low Reynolds number).
- **Autonomy + structure:** A flight-model ingest schema needs seam orientation as a state variable alongside spin axis/rate; without that field, SSW movement is unexplainable residual forever.
- **Second-order:** Pairs with A3/A4 (the notes and the fitted model) into a complete stack: phenomenon → rigid-body explanation → fitted detector.
- **Evidence:** explainer post; the underlying site is a research group's page. As-posted, UNVERIFIED at the paper level.

### A2. Seam-Shifted Wake deep dive: the sinker — @DrivelineBB (1323078218803408896) — LEARN-ONLY

- **What it is:** Driveline's long-form dive on how SSW interacts with the sinker — a pitch that largely faded from the game — covering how seam orientation can restore or kill the pitch's late arm-side run.
- **Citation/links:** Driveline blog (t.co link in-post did not expand on this route; the post is the pointer, the blog is the source).
- **How to use it / system mapping:** The applied counterpart to A1: pitch-design practice (grip/orientation choices) driven by wake physics. For GSE's physics lane it is the "what practitioners actually do with the theory" document.
- **Transfer beyond sports:** Same wake-management logic applies anywhere a spinning body's surface features can be deliberately oriented — a design lever, not just an analysis curiosity.
- **Autonomy + structure:** Pitch-design claims should be stored as orientation-conditioned movement deltas (with/without seam effect), not single movement numbers.
- **Second-order:** With A4's detector, closes the loop: theory → design intervention → measurement of who actually gets the effect.
- **Evidence:** blog-pointer post; content as-posted, UNVERIFIED.

### A3. Notes on spinning baseball physics (seam reorientation) — @sunshinevvn / Vivienne Pelletier (2029011711123177538) — LEARN-ONLY ★

- **What it is:** A 9-page self-published physics write-up asking why a pitch's seams appear to reorient under a seemingly static spin axis — worked through with real rigid-body dynamics: inertia tensor of a seamed ball, principal axes, torque-free precession of the spin axis about conserved angular momentum. This is the NumPy-PDF class of find: one person, deep math, no gate.
- **Citation/links:** GitHub repo `viviennep/spinning-baseball`; PDF: `spinning ball notes.pdf`. **PDF downloaded: `spinning-ball-notes.pdf`, 9 pp, read** (prerequisites + Question 1: precession from ω/principal-axis misalignment).
- **How to use it / system mapping:** The mechanism candidate underneath SSW-adjacent seam motion: if the spin axis precesses, seam orientation in the airflow changes through flight even with constant angular momentum. Directly feeds the fleet's trajectory-physics model as a state-evolution term.
- **Transfer beyond sports:** Torque-free precession of a slightly anisotropic rigid body is textbook-classical mechanics with live applications (satellite attitude, gyroscopes, any free-spinning manufactured object with imperfect symmetry).
- **Autonomy + structure:** A worked-example of the corpus standard: a claim decomposed into numbered questions with prerequisites stated — a template for how GSE's own physics notes should be structured for machine ingestion.
- **Second-order:** With A1, distinguishes two seam effects (wake tripping vs axis precession) that public discussion constantly conflates; the engine's physics layer should model them as separate terms.
- **Evidence:** PDF read at content level (first 3 pp + structure); author is a physics writer, not a journal venue — LEARN-ONLY on provenance, top-tier on substance.

### A4. Physics-based baseball flight model (Non-Magnus detector) — @georgerlewis (2106629621441503643) — TEST

- **What it is:** A working physics-based ball-flight model built on public data that flags pitches whose movement can't be explained by Magnus alone — candidate SSW pitches — with a modeled Brad Keller sinker as the worked example.
- **Citation/links:** post text only (model output image-carried; values gap flagged).
- **How to use it / system mapping:** The detector pattern: integrate the known physics, treat the residual as the measurement. TEST = reproduce the approach on public Statcast data and check whether flagged pitches match known SSW exemplars.
- **Transfer beyond sports:** Residual-as-measurement is a general system-identification method — fit the trusted model, mine the residual for the missing physics. Applies to any instrumented process.
- **Autonomy + structure:** Implies a two-layer flight schema: physics-predicted movement fields + residual fields, stored separately so the residual can be re-mined as models improve.
- **Second-order:** The same architecture is exactly how a Deserve-To-Win-style grading model should treat its own residuals (cf. the Oct 30 DTW reproduction).
- **Evidence:** as-posted; chart values image-locked, construction stated. UNVERIFIED.

### A5. The mathematics of golf ball flight dynamics — @mathematicsprof (1007594139272273920) — LEARN-ONLY

- **What it is:** A mathematics-of-flight treatment comparing golf ball dynamics with and without dimples — the drag-crisis story written as math: how dimples move the boundary-layer separation point and cut drag at flight Reynolds numbers.
- **Citation/links:** post is the pointer (no link survived the render; the author's golf-flight mathematics page is the source class).
- **How to use it / system mapping:** The cleanest classroom-grade statement of the drag crisis GSE's physics lane needs as reference math for any projectile work (EQ-PHYSICS family).
- **Transfer beyond sports:** The drag crisis is a general fluid-mechanics phenomenon (cylinders, spheres, cables, vehicle aerodynamics); the golf ball is just its most famous demo.
- **Autonomy + structure:** Reference-math dossiers like this belong in a citable constants/models layer (drag-coefficient-vs-Reynolds curves), not in prose notes.
- **Second-order:** Pairs with A8's CFD simulation (same phenomenon, computed rather than derived) — derivation + simulation cross-check is the fleet's verification pattern.
- **Evidence:** as-posted, UNVERIFIED; link gap flagged.

### A6. Orientation-dependent drag crisis of the World Cup ball — @FluidsMdpi (2115715267686985960) — LEARN-ONLY ★

- **What it is:** A peer-reviewed fluid-mechanics paper (MDPI *Fluids*, open access): "Orientation-Dependent Drag Crisis and Flight Response of the FIFA World Cup Match Ball Trionda" — wind-tunnel-grade analysis showing the match ball's drag crisis depends on panel orientation, so identical kicks fly differently by ball rotation state.
- **Citation/links:** *Fluids* 11(5):128, mdpi.com/2311-5521/11/5/128 (DOI via publisher page; journal site, open access).
- **How to use it / system mapping:** The soccer instance of A1's lesson: surface geometry is a flight variable. For the physics lane, it's the citable, peer-reviewed anchor for orientation-conditioned drag — the standard the baseball SSW literature is still catching up to.
- **Transfer beyond sports:** Directly: any paneled/textured projectile. Methodologically: orientation-resolved drag measurement is how all sports-ball aerodynamics should be reported.
- **Autonomy + structure:** Flight features should carry ball-orientation state; a single drag coefficient per ball model is a known-wrong simplification this paper quantifies.
- **Second-order:** World Cup ball redesigns are a recurring natural experiment — a standing watch item for the physics lane each cycle.
- **Evidence:** publisher page resolved; abstract-level read. As-posted numbers UNVERIFIED.

### A7. "The flight of a football" — spin and gyroscopic stability — @EaglesXsandOs (2087560609726697695) — LEARN-ONLY

- **What it is:** A research paper on American football flight, summarized in-post: more spin → more gyroscopic stability → the nose stays aligned with the velocity vector → drag stays low and the wake stays stable → longer, more consistent passes. The rare physics paper about *our* ball.
- **Citation/links:** paper not named in the rendered text (pointer gap flagged); the summary's mechanism chain is stated completely enough to search by.
- **How to use it / system mapping:** Directly on-brief for GSE: pass-flight physics underlies any throw-quality or ball-placement modeling from tracking data (release spin as a feature for completion/deep-ball outcomes).
- **Transfer beyond sports:** Gyroscopic stabilization of elongated projectiles is the artillery/rifle-ballistics problem; the football is a finless instance, which is what makes it interesting.
- **Autonomy + structure:** Tracking ingest should want release-spin estimates as first-class fields; a "spiral quality" feature is derivable from nose-alignment variance in tracking data.
- **Second-order:** Connects to QB evaluation: spiral consistency is a physical skill measurable from tracking, orthogonal to the arm-strength proxies the market uses.
- **Evidence:** as-posted summary, UNVERIFIED; paper identity unresolved on this route (gap audit).

### A8. Spinning golf ball CFD (TCAE) — @CFDSUPPORT (2113876456845812159) — LEARN-ONLY

- **What it is:** A CFD simulation study of a spinning golf ball at 40 m/s and 6,000 RPM (TCAE), visualizing Magnus effect and dimple-driven aerodynamic behavior, with a methods page on model setup and validation.
- **Citation/links:** cfdsupport.com/dimpled-ball-cfd (vendor methods page).
- **How to use it / system mapping:** The simulation counterpart to A5's derivations — a worked example of the full CFD pipeline (geometry, mesh, spin boundary condition, validation) applied to a sports ball.
- **Transfer beyond sports:** The pipeline is generic rotating-body CFD; the transferable asset is the validation discipline (compare against measured drag/lift before believing contours).
- **Autonomy + structure:** Any future in-house CFD claim should ship with the same artifact set: setup parameters + validation comparison, or it doesn't count as evidence.
- **Second-order:** Vendor content, so discounted — but its validation-first framing is the standard to hold A6-class papers to as well.
- **Evidence:** as-posted; vendor source. UNVERIFIED.

### A9. The physics behind freekicks and passing — @Soccermatics / David Sumpter (847739420501352449) — LEARN-ONLY

- **What it is:** Sumpter's physics-of-soccer explainer: the Magnus force on free kicks, knuckleball instability at low spin, and the geometry of passing lanes — written by the author of *Soccermatics*, the book that carried mathematical modeling into mainstream soccer analytics.
- **Citation/links:** soccermatics.medium.com article (full text public).
- **How to use it / system mapping:** The content-OS bridge: this is how physics gets explained to a general audience without losing the equations — a style reference for GSE's own public explainers.
- **Transfer beyond sports:** The knuckleball regime (near-zero spin, seam-driven chaotic wake) is the same instability class as A1/A3's seam physics — one phenomenon family across three sports.
- **Autonomy + structure:** Explainer structure worth copying in the content OS: phenomenon → equation → video example → implication.
- **Second-order:** Sumpter's passing-lane geometry is the conceptual ancestor of the pitch-control models in C5's lineage (EPV).
- **Evidence:** article resolved; as-posted.

### A10. QD-PINN: PINNs for Bayesian quickest detection — @StatsPapers (2115493705385656596) — LEARN-ONLY ★

- **What it is:** A 26-page arXiv paper (Ye, Liu, Xing) using physics-informed neural networks to estimate optimal stopping boundaries for quickest-detection problems in high-dimensional diffusions — PINNs solving the variational-inequality structure that blocks classical numerical methods.
- **Citation/links:** arXiv:2610.37473 (stat.ML/cs.LG). **PDF downloaded: `arxiv-2610.37473.pdf`, 26 pp; abstract read.**
- **How to use it / system mapping:** Quickest detection = optimal stopping under uncertainty = the mathematical form of "when do we know enough to act" — in GSE terms, live-game decision timing and alert triggering.
- **Transfer beyond sports:** This is the clearest transfer find in the tier: change-point detection in finance, medicine, and industrial monitoring is the same problem; the PINN machinery for variational inequalities is domain-free.
- **Autonomy + structure:** A candidate method for the fleet's monitoring layer (detect regime change in a data stream, then trigger review) with the stopping boundary learned, not hand-set.
- **Second-order:** Pairs with D-tier variational-inference papers: detection boundary + posterior calibration is a full decision pipeline.
- **Evidence:** arXiv metadata verified via API; abstract read. Results as-published, not reproduced.

### A11. Regional accuracy of PINNs for cavity recirculation — @PhysicsFluDyn (2116157577054548099) — LEARN-ONLY ★

- **What it is:** A 39-page arXiv paper (Roohi & McDevitt) stress-testing data-free PINNs on the deep lid-driven cavity — a Navier-Stokes benchmark — showing bulk-velocity agreement hides failure in the weak recirculation zones, and fixing assessment with regional collocation and regional error metrics.
- **Citation/links:** arXiv:2610.38814 (physics.flu-dyn). **PDF downloaded: `arxiv-2610.38814.pdf`, 39 pp; abstract read.**
- **How to use it / system mapping:** The evaluation lesson transfers to every GSE model: aggregate metrics certify the easy region and hide the failure region. This is the "check the weak zone" doctrine with a fluid-mechanics proof.
- **Transfer beyond sports:** Navier-Stokes-adjacent by construction — the cavity is the fruit-fly of CFD; regional-accuracy methodology applies to any PDE-surrogate validation, which is the ARGON-side physics program's core problem.
- **Autonomy + structure:** Evaluation harnesses should report worst-region error beside global error as a required field — a schema change this paper justifies on its own.
- **Second-order:** Directly relevant to Garrett's Navier-Stokes interest (MEMORY: NS harvest + EQ-PHYSICS-001): PINN surrogates for NS are only trustworthy with regional validation.
- **Evidence:** arXiv metadata verified; abstract read.

### A12. Unified trajectory and 3D pose forecasting for multi-agent sports — @CSVisionPapers (2114969491025125729) — TEST

- **What it is:** A WACV 2027 paper (Peral et al.) jointly forecasting player trajectories and 3D poses for multi-agent sports analysis — trajectory prediction and pose estimation in one model, from a CV-papers digest account.
- **Citation/links:** arXiv:2610.30519 (cs.CV). **PDF downloaded: `arxiv-2610.30519.pdf`, 11 pp.**
- **How to use it / system mapping:** The tracking-data frontier: GSE's matchup layer ultimately wants exactly this — where every player is going, in body coordinates. TEST = read the full paper and spec what a Big Data Bowl-data version would need.
- **Transfer beyond sports:** Multi-agent trajectory+pose forecasting is crowd dynamics, robotics, and autonomous-vehicle pedestrian prediction with different jerseys.
- **Autonomy + structure:** Joint trajectory/pose output is a schema: per-agent future positions + pose states at fixed horizons — the shape a tracking ingest should target.
- **Second-order:** With A15 (pose→style vectors) and A13 (open skeletal data), forms a complete open stack: data → representation → forecast.
- **Evidence:** arXiv resolved; PDF downloaded, abstract-level read.

### A13. Open skeletal tracking dataset (125 free throws) + mplbasketball — @devinpleuler (1833877692137037900) — ADOPT-CANDIDATE

- **What it is:** Sport Performance Lab's first open-source dataset release: skeletal tracking from 125 free throws captured on a markerless system, shipped with the `mplbasketball` package to load, evaluate, and visualize player motion.
- **Citation/links:** post text (dataset/package names given; repo via the lab's public channels).
- **How to use it / system mapping:** A complete, tiny, honest biomechanics dataset with tooling — the right size for a first in-house pose/tracking pipeline test before touching NFL tracking. ADOPT-CANDIDATE for the fleet's tracking lab.
- **Transfer beyond sports:** Markerless pose pipelines transfer to rehab, ergonomics, and coaching in any movement domain.
- **Autonomy + structure:** The release pattern itself is the lesson: data + loader package + visualization in one drop — the standard GSE's own dataset releases should meet.
- **Second-order:** Free-throw skeletal data + A12's pose forecasting = a self-contained experiment: forecast the shot outcome from pose trajectory.
- **Evidence:** as-posted; dataset existence as-posted, UNVERIFIED at file level.

### A14. Accuracy and precision of kinematic methods for ball release — Jinji, Slowik, Fleisig (@DYbrand) (2115817715902632161) — LEARN-ONLY

- **What it is:** A *Sports Biomechanics* paper measuring how accurately different kinematic methods pin down the moment of ball release in pitching — metrology for the tracking pipeline itself: Fleisig is the ASMI biomechanics line, the field's measurement backbone.
- **Citation/links:** Sports Biomechanics, DOI 10.1080/14763141.2026.2746454 (Taylor & Francis, full-text link in-post).
- **How to use it / system mapping:** Every tracking-derived feature inherits error from event-timing definitions (release, foot plant, break). This is the error-budget literature: before modeling, know the measurement noise floor of the input events.
- **Transfer beyond sports:** Event-timing metrology is universal in sensor pipelines — the same question appears in gait analysis, robotics, and industrial motion capture.
- **Autonomy + structure:** Feature schemas should carry a measurement-error field sourced from metrology papers like this, so models can weight features by their noise floor.
- **Second-order:** Directly disciplines A12/A13 enthusiasm: pose data is only as good as its event definitions.
- **Evidence:** publisher page resolved; as-posted.

### A15. Pose-tracking → joint trajectories → player style vectors — @ADLXBT (2114249536189190302) — WATCH

- **What it is:** A builder's note on an off-policy pipeline: pose tracking produces joint trajectories, a neural net learns a per-player style vector from them, and the author names the missing link themselves — the model that maps style vectors to predictive insights.
- **Citation/links:** post text only.
- **How to use it / system mapping:** The representation-learning bet for tracking data: compress a player's movement into a vector, then scout/cluster/predict on vectors. WATCH because the hard half (vectors → predictions) is admittedly unbuilt — the honest gap is the information.
- **Transfer beyond sports:** Style vectors from motion are digital-twin representations — same pattern in rehab monitoring, animation, and worker-safety analytics.
- **Autonomy + structure:** If GSE builds player embeddings from tracking, this post is the cautionary spec: embedding first, prediction head second, and the interface between them is the research problem.
- **Second-order:** A12 gives the forecasting machinery; A15 gives the representation; A13 gives open data to test the marriage.
- **Evidence:** as-posted builder note, UNVERIFIED.

## B. NFL modeling core — the engine's home territory (18)

### B1. Everything I know about making an NFL win probability model (xgboost, R) — @benbbaldwin (1383828904855494656) — ADOPT-CANDIDATE

- **What it is:** Baldwin's full write-up for Open Source Football on building an NFL win probability model with xgboost in R — features, training design, and the calibration discipline behind the nflfastR WP model the whole public ecosystem uses.
- **Citation/links:** Open Source Football post (t.co in-post; opensourcefootball.com).
- **How to use it / system mapping:** The canonical reference implementation for GSE's WP layer: any in-house WP work should be diffed against this document's choices (feature set, time/decay handling, calibration checks) before claiming an improvement.
- **Transfer beyond sports:** Gradient-boosted probability estimation with honest calibration is the generic recipe for any binary-outcome live forecasting (churn, default, failure prediction).
- **Autonomy + structure:** A model card pattern: features, training window, calibration plot, known failure modes — the fields this post models are the fields GSE's model registry should require.
- **Second-order:** With B2/B3, it's the complete Baldwin stack: data+models (nflfastR), decisions (nfl4th), and the WP method itself.
- **Evidence:** as-posted; the linked write-up is a known public artifact of the nflverse ecosystem.

### B2. nflfastR 2.0 — EPA, CPOE, WP models release — @benbbaldwin (1272567417542250496) — ADOPT-CANDIDATE

- **What it is:** The release post for nflfastR 2.0: play-by-play back to 1999 with new and improved EPA, CPOE, and win probability models, compatible with the new NFL data sources.
- **Citation/links:** nflfastR package (nflverse).
- **How to use it / system mapping:** Historical but load-bearing: this is the release that standardized the exact EP/WP/CPOE definitions GSE reproduces against. The corpus's reproduction work (signal pack, Oct 30) stands on these definitions — including their known quirks (cf. EXTRA100 A7: nflverse success-rate is EPA-derived).
- **Transfer beyond sports:** A versioning lesson: when the reference implementation changes its models, every downstream number changes; provenance must record model version beside every stored metric.
- **Autonomy + structure:** GSE's ingest should stamp every EPA/WP/CPOE value with the producing package + version — this post is why.
- **Second-order:** Pairs with F-tier nflverse release posts (B17) as the data-supply chain the engine depends on.
- **Evidence:** release post; as-posted.

### B3. nfl4th — 4th-down and conversion recommendations package — @benbbaldwin (1372227225559437317) — ADOPT-CANDIDATE

- **What it is:** The nfl4th R package: computes expected win probability for {go, punt, FG} and {go for 1, go for 2} from any game state, with season-loading for analysis.
- **Citation/links:** nfl4th package (t.co in-post).
- **How to use it / system mapping:** A decision layer already built on B1/B2's models — the reference for GSE's situations layer (weekly cycle step 4). Any in-house 4th-down tool must beat or match this one's transparency to justify existing.
- **Transfer beyond sports:** Expected-value decision support under a fitted outcome model is the generic form (medicine, operations, finance): the package is a clean instance of model → decision table.
- **Autonomy + structure:** Decision outputs should ship as full option tables with WP per option (as nfl4th does), never a bare recommendation — that's what makes the decision auditable.
- **Second-order:** B14 (run_the_sims) is its mirror: nfl4th computes what coaches *should* do; the sportsbook model predicts what they *will* do. The gap between the two tables is itself a feature (coach tendency).
- **Evidence:** release post; as-posted.

### B4. Hierarchical field-goal model on 23,247 kicks — @nfl_simulator (2095240087717986628) — LEARN-ONLY

- **What it is:** The simulator's kicking model: a hierarchical model fit on 23,247 field goals with distance, wind, temperature, elevation, roof, and kicker effects; league make rates stated in-post (97% from 30, 80% from 45, 61% from 55 — as-posted).
- **Citation/links:** model write-up via the account's Medium (companion post in the same run).
- **How to use it / system mapping:** A complete spec for the kicking component the Oct 30 Deserve-To-Win reproduction found MISSING from the shipped simulator v2 (v1 had it, via paid data). This post is the public blueprint for rebuilding that component in-house: hierarchical partial pooling per kicker with weather covariates.
- **Transfer beyond sports:** Hierarchical models with environmental covariates are the standard for any repeated human-performance measurement (the kicker is interchangeable with a machine, a clinician, a fund manager).
- **Autonomy + structure:** Covariate list is a schema: distance + wind + temp + elevation + roof + entity random effect. GSE's kicking features should match it field-for-field.
- **Second-order:** With B5, shows the same author's shrinkage discipline across two model components — a coherent modeling philosophy worth adopting wholesale.
- **Evidence:** as-posted numbers UNVERIFIED; construction fully stated.

### B5. Interception shrinkage — Denver's 77% catch rate → ~55% estimate — @nfl_simulator (2095240387472273741) — LEARN-ONLY

- **What it is:** A two-post receipt: the model shrunk Denver's 2024 interceptable-throw catch rate (13/17, 77%) to ~55%; in 2025 Denver caught 8/21 (38%), so the shrunk estimate beat the raw rate. Published prediction, published outcome.
- **Citation/links:** post text (self-contained).
- **How to use it / system mapping:** The grading lane's ideal artifact: a shrinkage claim with its own follow-up verification. This is how every GSE regression-to-mean claim should be stored — estimate, shrinkage target, and the next-season check.
- **Transfer beyond sports:** Empirical-Bayes shrinkage of small-sample rates is universal (hospitals, sales reps, A/B tests); the receipt format is the transferable part.
- **Autonomy + structure:** Claims registry pattern: every model claim gets a scheduled re-check against future data, exactly as this author did manually.
- **Second-order:** Complements the corpus's luck accounting (S107 family): shrinkage handles the rate stats, WP-swing handles the plays.
- **Evidence:** as-posted; the 2025 follow-up is stated in the same post. UNVERIFIED independently.

### B6. Separating OL and QB priors for pressure and sacks — @Timo_NFL (1672229769541238784) — LEARN-ONLY

- **What it is:** A model that separates offensive-line and QB priors for pressure and sack rates, then simulates a QB behind different lines (Fields behind CHI's line: 43% pressure / 11.4% sacks; behind PHI's: 33% / 10.5% — as-posted). The stated doctrine: pressure belongs more to the OL, sacks more to the QB.
- **Citation/links:** post text (chart companion image-locked).
- **How to use it / system mapping:** The attribution structure the matchups layer needs for pressure: two priors, one interaction. Directly extends the PocketContext decomposition (S113, EXTRA100 A1) — that post decomposes the *rates*; this one decomposes the *responsibility*.
- **Transfer beyond sports:** Credit assignment between a system and an operator (line vs QB) is the same statistics problem as machine vs operator, school vs teacher, fund vs manager.
- **Autonomy + structure:** Priors should be stored as separate entities with their own update rules — a schema note for the engine's player/team prior tables.
- **Second-order:** With B9 (survival-curve sack credit), two independent public methods now divide sack blame; an in-house bake-off is specifiable from these two posts alone.
- **Evidence:** as-posted values UNVERIFIED; construction stated.

### B7. How playoff leverage is actually computed — @Timo_NFL (2113644455530057926) — LEARN-ONLY

- **What it is:** A one-post methods disclosure: playoff leverage is usually computed by taking simulation results, filtering to the win branch and the loss branch for a team, and differencing playoff probability between the two filtered sets — not by re-simming counterfactual worlds.
- **Citation/links:** post text (self-contained).
- **How to use it / system mapping:** Leverage is a headline GSE number (the corpus has chased Timo's leverage chart across three sweeps, image-locked every time). This post gives the estimator in words: filter-and-difference on one simulation ensemble. It also exposes the estimator's weakness — it conditions on the realized branch, so it inherits simulation noise in both arms.
- **Transfer beyond sports:** Branch-difference on a Monte Carlo ensemble is the generic way to price any single event's importance (a sales call, a court ruling, a product launch).
- **Autonomy + structure:** Any leverage figure GSE publishes should carry its estimator definition and ensemble size; this post is the definition to cite or improve on.
- **Second-order:** An obvious improvement the post invites: common-random-numbers pairing between branches to cut the noise — a concrete methods upgrade for the sim lane.
- **Evidence:** as-posted; construction stated plainly.

### B8. Bayesian QB rankings with methodology primer — @kevin_cole (2086815616032256102) — LEARN-ONLY

- **What it is:** Preseason QB rankings at Unexpected Points built on Bayesian statistical analysis, shipped with a methodology primer covering mean projection vs range-of-outcomes and a comparison against market-adjacent tiers (Sando's).
- **Citation/links:** Unexpected Points (link in replies, not in the rendered text — pointer gap flagged).
- **How to use it / system mapping:** The rankings are the least of it; the primer's mean-vs-range framing is the asset — a public example of publishing a distribution, not a point, which is the engine's calibration doctrine in editorial form.
- **Transfer beyond sports:** Any ranked forecast (employees, products, regions) inherits the same duty: publish the range or admit the ranking is theater.
- **Autonomy + structure:** Ranking artifacts should carry per-entity posterior summaries (mean + interval) as required fields.
- **Second-order:** Cole's Bayesian frame + B6's priors + B4's hierarchy are the same statistical religion at three scales — together they define the house style GSE's methods should converge on.
- **Evidence:** as-posted; primer existence as-posted.

### B9. Sack adjustment via survival curves — @kevin_cole (1844018663491502586) — LEARN-ONLY

- **What it is:** A sack-credit method: survival-curve analysis of time-to-pressure/time-to-sack divides blame between QBs who hold the ball too long and lines that allow pressure too quickly, using FTN charting; the model's verdict on Brissett (responsible for most of his own elevated sack rate) stated in-post.
- **Citation/links:** post text; data: FTN charting (as-posted).
- **How to use it / system mapping:** Survival analysis is the right mathematics for time-to-event pressure data — a method class the engine doesn't currently use. This post is the spec seed: hazard of pressure over dropback time, with QB and OL as competing covariates.
- **Transfer beyond sports:** Survival/hazard modeling transfers to churn, failure, and recovery processes everywhere; the credit-division framing (who caused the event) is causal attribution on hazard scales.
- **Autonomy + structure:** Time-to-pressure should be ingested as a distribution (or hazard curve) per QB/OL pairing, not a single average — the average destroys exactly what this method uses.
- **Second-order:** The natural opponent/complement to B6's prior-separation model; comparing their blame assignments on the same QBs is a ready-made methods audit.
- **Evidence:** as-posted, UNVERIFIED.

### B10. Building 4th-down models for a sportsbook: predict the coach, not the optimum — @run_the_sims (2112909488885748089) — LEARN-ONLY ★

- **What it is:** A practitioner disclosure: at a sportsbook, 4th-down models exist to predict what coaches *will* do (they go for it only 29% of the time the model says to — as-posted), because prices need behavior, not advice. Includes coach-tendency findings (Shanahan: aggressive when obvious, conservative when marginal; 3rd most conservative since 2014 — as-posted).
- **Citation/links:** post text (thread).
- **How to use it / system mapping:** The behavior-vs-optimum distinction is a first-class modeling fork for GSE's situations layer: in-game win probability needs the *behavioral* branch probabilities (what the coach will actually choose), while content/grading wants the *optimal* table. One model silently serving both is a category error this post names.
- **Transfer beyond sports:** Modeling agents as they are vs as they should be is the central split in policy prediction, market making, and negotiation analytics.
- **Autonomy + structure:** Decision models should declare their type (descriptive vs prescriptive) in the model card; mixing them is how silent errors enter.
- **Second-order:** With B3 (nfl4th), the two tables side by side define a "coach tax" feature: WP lost per game by behavior-vs-optimum divergence.
- **Evidence:** as-posted numbers UNVERIFIED; practitioner provenance stated.

### B11. A luck index built one play at a time — @run_the_sims (2110392653992919449) — LEARN-ONLY

- **What it is:** A play-level luck index (WP swing on plays the team didn't control — dropped interceptions, fumble bounces, blocked kicks), with the season's unluckiest plays enumerated and the method pointed to in-thread; data: nflverse PBP + FTN charting.
- **Citation/links:** post text; methods in the thread's 3rd post.
- **How to use it / system mapping:** A second public luck accounting beside the corpus's S107 construction (run_the_sims' own Luck+Penalties) and DataWithBliss's dashboard (EXTRA100 A5) — three constructions of the same concept now on file, which is exactly what a methods comparison needs. Grading lane only: luck explains results, it never forms plays.
- **Transfer beyond sports:** Outcome-vs-process separation via counterfactual WP is general performance evaluation: any domain with a win-probability-like state function can build the same index.
- **Autonomy + structure:** Luck components should be stored per play with the WP model's version stamped (same lesson as B2).
- **Second-order:** The author's own caveat tradition (EXTRA100 captured his confounding note) makes this the most honestly-documented of the three luck systems.
- **Evidence:** as-posted values UNVERIFIED; construction stated.

### B18. You can't take EPA at face value: tail parameters and scheme — @KentWeyrauch (2115790559965044747) — LEARN-ONLY

- **What it is:** A methods critique in post form: EPA is scheme-agnostic by construction, its error bars are overlaid on an average NFL scheme, and pushing an offense to an extreme pushes opposing defenses to extremes too — so EPA comparisons at the tails are model-assumption-sensitive in a way the headline number hides.
- **Citation/links:** post text (self-contained).
- **How to use it / system mapping:** The standing caution for the engine's core metric, from inside the community: EPA is a model, its tails are extrapolations, and strategic response (defenses adjusting) breaks the ceteris paribus the metric assumes. Belongs in the engine's model-risk documentation next to the Oct 30 reproduction standards.
- **Transfer beyond sports:** "The metric assumes the environment doesn't react to being measured" is Goodhart-adjacent and universal — every KPI in every domain has this failure mode at the tails.
- **Autonomy + structure:** Metric records should carry an applicability-range field (where the model was fit vs where it's being applied).
- **Second-order:** Pairs with C8 (Meta-Analytics) as the two ends of metric skepticism: one philosophical, one statistical.
- **Evidence:** as-posted argument; no numbers to verify.

## C. Sports analytics research — the published record (14)

### C1. Hierarchical Bayesian joint modeling of athlete performance trajectories and latent peak ages — Peng, Guan, Zhao (@mathSTb) (2115144534904431011) — LEARN-ONLY ★

- **What it is:** A 30-page statistics paper (stat.AP/stat.ME) jointly modeling athletes' performance trajectories and their latent peak ages in a hierarchical Bayesian framework — aging curves with partial pooling, where "peak age" is a latent variable to be inferred, not assumed.
- **Citation/links:** arXiv:2610.35473. **PDF downloaded: `arxiv-2610.35473.pdf`, 30 pp.**
- **How to use it / system mapping:** Aging curves sit underneath every player projection GSE makes (usage layer, weekly cycle step 1). A joint trajectory+peak-age model is the principled replacement for hand-tuned age adjustments: player priors that know where each athlete is on his own curve.
- **Transfer beyond sports:** Latent-peak trajectory modeling is human-capital analytics (careers, skill acquisition, rehabilitation progress) with sport as the measured instance.
- **Autonomy + structure:** Projection features should carry trajectory posterior summaries (current level, slope, peak-age posterior) — three fields this paper defines.
- **Second-order:** With B8's Bayesian rankings primer, gives both the editorial and the formal version of distribution-first player evaluation.
- **Evidence:** arXiv resolved via post link; PDF downloaded. Results as-published.

### C2. Graph-to-Grid (G2G): continuous-coordinate feature painting for soccer pass surfaces — Günay & Gun (@Memoirs) (2103178554250768663) — TEST

- **What it is:** A 39-page paper replacing rasterized per-cell count features with interpolated continuous-coordinate "painting" of player features onto pitch grids — pass surfaces that keep *where inside the cell* each player stands. The abstract's framing is the lesson: rasterization quietly destroys the information the model then can't use.
- **Citation/links:** arXiv:2609.24040 (cs.LG/cs.CV). **PDF downloaded: `arxiv-2609.24040.pdf`, 39 pp; abstract read.**
- **How to use it / system mapping:** Every spatial feature GSE builds from tracking (coverage shells, separation surfaces, pressure maps) faces the same rasterization choice. TEST = apply the G2G interpolation idea to one existing grid feature and measure whether the model can tell the difference.
- **Transfer beyond sports:** Entity-to-grid feature transfer is LiDAR perception, weather modeling, and epidemiology mapping — the paper explicitly benchmarks against those fields' methods.
- **Autonomy + structure:** Feature-store entries should record their spatial encoding (raster vs interpolated) as provenance; silent information loss is a schema bug, and this paper names it.
- **Second-order:** Pairs with A12 (trajectory+pose forecasting): G2G is the input representation that kind of model deserves.
- **Evidence:** arXiv metadata verified via API; abstract read.

### C3. Topological data analysis + graph theory for tennis prediction — Schwaderer et al. (@Memoirs) (2079547616203870556) — LEARN-ONLY

- **What it is:** A 19-page paper predicting ATP match outcomes (2000–2025) from the *shape* of the player competitive network: persistent homology over lower-star filtrations (four topological summary methods) plus modified band depth — features no box score contains.
- **Citation/links:** arXiv:2607.23509 (cs.LG/math.AT/stat.ML). **PDF downloaded: `arxiv-2607.23509.pdf`, 19 pp; abstract read.**
- **How to use it / system mapping:** The one-of-a-kind find of this section: head-to-head networks have topology, and topology carries signal about the field's structure (eras, clusters, bridge players). For GSE, the analog is team/player interaction networks across a season.
- **Transfer beyond sports:** TDA on competitive networks transfers to any tournament/market/ecology of pairwise contests; persistent homology is the domain-free part.
- **Autonomy + structure:** Introduces a new feature family (topological summaries) that no current GSE schema anticipates — exactly why the corpus keeps a "may do more later" status rather than a verdict of irrelevance.
- **Second-order:** With C7's Sloan inventory (which includes network-flavored work like Counterpoints), traces a decade-long arc of structure-over-statistics methods.
- **Evidence:** arXiv metadata verified; abstract read.

### C4. Optimising football corner tactics with graph reinforcement learning — Groom et al. (@Memoirs) (2064192362373431576) — LEARN-ONLY

- **What it is:** An 11-page paper formulating corner-kick design as a decision problem: a central policy adjusts player configurations to *discover* generalisable routines rather than imitate historical ones — RL as a tactics inventor, not a tactics classifier.
- **Citation/links:** arXiv:2606.06353 (cs.LG). **PDF downloaded: `arxiv-2606.06353.pdf`, 11 pp; abstract read.**
- **How to use it / system mapping:** The NFL analog is immediate: red-zone and 2-point play design as configuration optimization. The paper's framing (beyond imitation → discovery) is the line between GSE's descriptive models and a prescriptive play-design engine.
- **Transfer beyond sports:** Configuration optimization under a learned simulator is robot formation control, warehouse layout, and team-roster design — the graph-RL machinery is portable.
- **Autonomy + structure:** Prescriptive outputs need a different gate than predictive ones: a discovered tactic ships with simulator provenance and a discovery-vs-imitation label.
- **Second-order:** With B10 (behavior vs optimum), forms the full decision stack: predict what coaches do, compute what they should do, invent what nobody does yet.
- **Evidence:** arXiv metadata verified; abstract read.

### C5. Expected Possession Value, full technical version — Bornn, Cervone, et al. (@JaviOnData) (1337482711460704259) — ADOPT-CANDIDATE ★

- **What it is:** The full technical arXiv version of Expected Possession Value for soccer with tracking data — the EPV framework (multiresolution spatial models of possession value) that is the direct intellectual ancestor of football's EP models. 39 pages, by the Stats Perform / Bornn lab line.
- **Citation/links:** arXiv:2012.05757. **PDF downloaded: `arxiv-2012.05757.pdf`, 39 pp.**
- **How to use it / system mapping:** GSE's EP model descends from this family; having the full technical version on file (not the conference summary) means the lineage's assumptions — spatial smoothing scales, action-value decomposition — are checkable against the engine's own choices. ADOPT-CANDIDATE as the canonical methods reference for the EP layer.
- **Transfer beyond sports:** Possession-value surfaces are state-value functions; the multiresolution technique is spatial statistics applicable to any valued-territory process.
- **Autonomy + structure:** Methods references belong in the corpus at full text, not as citations — this download is the standard.
- **Second-order:** Cervone's basketball EPV and Bornn's NFL injury work (C7 inventory) make this one node in a documented research program GSE can learn from wholesale.
- **Evidence:** arXiv resolved; PDF downloaded.

### C6. Mapping Academic Sports Analytics: content analysis of JQAS and JSA — Nathaniel (@StatAP_papers) (2114680897853112581) — LEARN-ONLY

- **What it is:** A 23-page bibliometric paper (stat.AP) content-analyzing the field's two journals (Journal of Quantitative Analysis in Sports; Journal of Sports Analytics) — what the academic field actually publishes, by topic and method, over time.
- **Citation/links:** arXiv:2610.32354. **PDF downloaded: `arxiv-2610.32354.pdf`, 23 pp.**
- **How to use it / system mapping:** A map of the territory this whole pull hunts in: which methods the journals reward, which sports are over/under-studied. For a research-and-data company, this is market research on its own field.
- **Transfer beyond sports:** Bibliometric self-mapping is how any research program audits its own blind spots; the method transfers even where the subject doesn't.
- **Autonomy + structure:** Direct input to sweep design: the paper's topic distribution is a checklist for future keyword programs (which methods are we *not* searching for?).
- **Second-order:** Read against this pull's own yield (heavy Bayesian/GP/VI, thin OR/decision-science) to find the corpus's current skew.
- **Evidence:** arXiv resolved via post link; PDF downloaded.

### C7. Eleven Sloan papers in five years: the Bornn lab inventory — @LukeBornn (965986388863631360) — LEARN-ONLY

- **What it is:** Bornn's own inventory of his lab's 11 MIT Sloan papers 2014–2018: EPV ('14), Counterpoints and Move or Die ('15), Pressing Game and Court Realty ('16), Possession Sketches and Scorekeeper Bias ('17), Open Spaces, NFL Injury, NBA Replay, and Deep Learning Trajectories ('18) — a one-post syllabus of the tracking-data revolution.
- **Citation/links:** post text (summary thread below it).
- **How to use it / system mapping:** Each title is a methods family GSE uses or should: possession value, defensive positioning value, injury modeling, trajectory learning. The inventory works as a reading list with provenance — and note "NFL Injury" and "Scorekeeper Bias" (measurement bias in the data itself) are the two most directly GSE-relevant.
- **Transfer beyond sports:** Scorekeeper bias — the data-generating process has a human in it with incentives — is a universal measurement warning (medical coding, police reports, sales CRM).
- **Autonomy + structure:** Research-program inventories like this are how the corpus should organize people: by program, not by post.
- **Second-order:** C5 is one paper from this inventory at full length; the other ten are now named targets for the PDF lane.
- **Evidence:** as-posted inventory; titles as-posted.

### C8. Meta-Analytics: tools for understanding the statistical properties of sports metrics — @XY_Research (782754772604424192) — TEST

- **What it is:** A paper announcement for "Meta-Analytics" (the XY Research line — the group behind several foundational sports-stats methods papers): tools for studying the statistical properties *of the metrics themselves* — reliability, validity, and noise structure of the numbers analytics runs on.
- **Citation/links:** post text (paper title given; venue via the XY line's publication record — pointer-level).
- **How to use it / system mapping:** The metric-audit layer: before a metric enters the engine, meta-analytics asks how much of it is signal. TEST = apply its reliability framing to one GSE feature family and report the signal fraction. This is the statistical twin of B18's philosophical caution.
- **Transfer beyond sports:** Fully transferable: every KPI program in every industry needs meta-analytics; sports just published it first.
- **Autonomy + structure:** Feature registry should carry reliability estimates (split-half / test-retest) as required fields — this paper is the citation for why.
- **Second-order:** With the Oct 30 signal-pack checks (which found a diverged public table), forms the corpus's evidence that un-audited metrics mislead by default.
- **Evidence:** as-posted title/provenance; full text not read (gap).

### C9. Converting college football spread differentials to probabilities — Sides & Harvill (@btbanalytics1) (2109706892620734900) — LEARN-ONLY

- **What it is:** A 12-page paper giving a method to convert NCAA point-spread differentials into win probabilities (normal-distribution margin model adjusted so common differentials get proper mass), posted as part of a worked pricing recipe with nfelo's margin-probability method and CollegeFootballData.
- **Citation/links:** arXiv:2212.08116 (Sides & Harvill, 2022). **PDF downloaded: `arxiv-2212.08116.pdf`, 12 pp; abstract read.**
- **How to use it / system mapping:** Books-last doctrine makes this a price-check tool only: spread→probability conversion is how the engine should read a market price when it finally looks at one. The discrete-mass correction (key numbers) is the part naive normal conversions get wrong.
- **Transfer beyond sports:** Converting an expert/market quote into a calibrated probability via historical outcome distributions is generic forecast translation.
- **Autonomy + structure:** The post itself is a workflow (method + data + tool chained) — a reminder that value often sits in the composition, which sweep dossiers should record as a unit.
- **Second-order:** Pairs with F2 (arbitrage paper) on the market-microstructure shelf; both are context for pricing, never pick formation.
- **Evidence:** arXiv metadata verified; abstract read.

### C10. Hockey game-performance model methodology — @JFreshHockey (2115520702115639698) — LEARN-ONLY

- **What it is:** A methodology disclosure from a public hockey modeler: after experimenting with many formulations, the model emphasizes concrete goal impacts (creation, penalties) over on-ice xG at single-game level, explicitly because that lined outputs up with real game results.
- **Citation/links:** post text (self-contained methodology note).
- **How to use it / system mapping:** A model-design confession worth filing: the target definition (what the model is *for* — single-game description vs season prediction) changed the winning feature set. GSE's per-game vs season models face the identical fork.
- **Transfer beyond sports:** "Define the decision the model serves before choosing features" is universal modeling hygiene; hockey is just where this post says it out loud.
- **Autonomy + structure:** Model cards need a "serving decision" field; this post is the cautionary example for its absence.
- **Second-order:** Cross-sport methodology posts like this are the cheapest R&D in the corpus — another sport's solved argument, imported free.
- **Evidence:** as-posted, UNVERIFIED.

### C11. The Expected Goals Method (thread) — @JamesTippett (1011268751579283457) — LEARN-ONLY

- **What it is:** The classic xG explainer thread by the author of *The Expected Goals Philosophy*: what xG is, why syndicates (Smartodds) and clubs (Brentford) built edges on it, and why media/fans misunderstand it — the method's public founding document.
- **Citation/links:** post text (thread).
- **How to use it / system mapping:** xG is soccer's EP; the thread is the clearest short statement of the expected-value-of-a-state philosophy that GSE's entire EP/WP stack instantiates. Content-OS value too: this is how a metric gets explained to a skeptical public.
- **Transfer beyond sports:** The philosophy — judge decisions by the distribution they create, not the outcome that happened — is the core of every process-over-results system, including GSE's grading lane.
- **Autonomy + structure:** None direct; filed as doctrine reference.
- **Second-order:** With C12, the book-length version of the same math (expected *points* from xG) is also in this pull — thread for the idea, book for the derivation.
- **Evidence:** as-posted.

### C12. The technical maths behind Expected Points — @xGPhilosophy (1439998364821639181) — LEARN-ONLY

- **What it is:** A pointer to the technical appendix of *The Expected Goals Philosophy*: how match-level Expected Points (xP) are computed from xG — each team accumulates 0.00–3.00 xP based on how one-sided the match was from an xG standpoint (as-posted summary of the method).
- **Citation/links:** book (The Expected Goals Philosophy); summary in-post.
- **How to use it / system mapping:** xP is the bridge from per-event value (xG) to per-match outcome distribution — the same two-level structure as EPA → WP in football. The computation (simulate/sum the goal distribution) is the part worth having precisely.
- **Transfer beyond sports:** Two-level value aggregation (event value → outcome distribution) is the generic form of any points-based competition or SLA scoring system.
- **Autonomy + structure:** Aggregation methods belong in the methods library at formula level; this dossier records the pointer and the headline form.
- **Second-order:** Direct ancestor logic for Deserve-To-Win-style grading (a postgame xP for football is a DTW sibling).
- **Evidence:** as-posted summary; book not read (gap).

### C13. Bradley-Terry rankings can flip: validity and statistical power in ranking — @ApurvaBadithela (2114852021543047549) — TEST

- **What it is:** A research thread (post 2 of a series) arguing prior ranking approaches fail two ways: they don't produce *valid* rankings, or they lack statistical power — Bradley-Terry specifically always emits a complete ranking, but that ranking can flip as more evaluations arrive.
- **Citation/links:** post text (thread; the underlying work is the author's ranking-research line).
- **How to use it / system mapping:** Bradley-Terry is inside GSE's rating-engines skill set — a validity critique of BT from a researcher working on ranking is a direct input: when is a complete ranking a lie? TEST = read the full thread/work and check the engine's BT usage against its conditions.
- **Transfer beyond sports:** Ranking validity is the LLM-evaluation problem (arenas run on BT) and every leaderboard's problem; this is front-line methodology for the whole AI field right now.
- **Autonomy + structure:** Ranking outputs should carry stability diagnostics (how the order moves under resampling) — this thread supplies the argument and the author to follow for the method.
- **Second-order:** D22 (DPO's BT foundations) is the same model in another costume; the corpus now holds both the use and the critique.
- **Evidence:** as-posted thread fragment; full thread not read (gap).

### C14. Dixon–Coles v2 stays the production baseline (375-fixture test) — @EsigboneO (2115734968739529205) — LEARN-ONLY

- **What it is:** A modeler's test report in one post: across 375 fixtures, a combined model scored best on average, but gains were uncertain — so Dixon–Coles v2 remains the production baseline. An honest negative result, publicly filed.
- **Citation/links:** post text (self-contained).
- **How to use it / system mapping:** Two assets: (1) Dixon–Coles remains the reference for goal-based score modeling — GSE's rating-engines skill covers it, and this is fresh evidence of its durability against ML challengers; (2) the *behavior* — challenger wins on average, uncertainty acknowledged, baseline kept — is exactly the promotion discipline GSE's gate doctrine demands.
- **Transfer beyond sports:** "Best on average, gains uncertain, keep the baseline" is correct model governance everywhere; the post is a one-line case study.
- **Autonomy + structure:** Promotion decisions need the uncertainty statement attached to the comparison, or the comparison doesn't count — this post models that.
- **Second-order:** With C13, the ratings shelf now has its two failure modes documented: invalid rankings (BT) and unprovable improvements (DC challengers).
- **Evidence:** as-posted, UNVERIFIED.
